[p1] How many heat invariants determine a hyperbolic
[p1]                   orbifold?

[p1]                                       Abstract
[p1]   The short-time expansion of the heat trace of a surface records its geometry
[p1]   order
[p1]   √      by order in t, a cone point of order m acting out to distances of about
[p1]     t/ sin(π/m). For closed orientable 2-orbifolds of curvature −1 whose singular
[p1]   points are cone points, we ask how many coefficients of this expansion, the heat
[p1]   invariants c1 , c2 , . . . , determine the genus and the cone orders. If every cone
[p1]   order is at most M , the first M + 1 heat invariants suffice at every area, and no
[p1]   smaller number works for all areas, so the number can grow only through large
[p1]   cone orders. √
[p1]                Without that bound the number needed at area at most A grows
[p1]   at least like A and at most linearly, and a lower bound of the form cAα is
[p1]   equivalent to the bound N (k) = O(k1/α ) for the least size N (k) of a solution
[p1]   of degree k of the Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem; linear growth is equivalent to
[p1]                                           1
[p1] 
[p2]      the open problem N (k) = O(k). Four heat invariants determine the signature
[p2]      of a triangle orbifold among all these orbifolds, and three determine a triangle
[p2]      orbifold among triangle orbifolds. Two first fail on O(2, 8, 8) and O(3, 3, 12),
[p2]      the unique pair of least area in the whole class sharing two invariants; among
[p2]      triangle orbifolds this pair and its multiples share their first two invariants with
[p2]      no third, because the cubic curve that carries such collisions is here an elliptic
[p2]      curve of rank 0. The counts are a constant-curvature phenomenon: an exactly
[p2]      flat cone point contributes to c2 only, and with variable curvature the minimal
[p2]      pair carries metrics, flat near the cone points, whose heat invariants agree to all
[p2]      orders. The coefficients describe diffusion only in the limit t → 0: the proved
[p2]      recoveries of c3 from values of the heat trace use shorter times than computed
[p2]      spectra resolve.
[p2]      Keywords: Heat invariants, Hyperbolic orbifolds, Inverse spectral problems, Cone
[p2]      singularities, Power sums, Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem
[p2]      MSC Classification: 58J53 (primary); 58J50 , 35K08 , 57R18 , 30F35 , 11D72 , 11G05
[p2] 1 Introduction
[p2] Put a unit of heat at a point of a closed surface and let it spread. For a short time t
[p2] the heat kernel on the diagonal depends, up to an error smaller than every power of t,
[p2] only on the geometry in a fixed neighbourhood of the point  √ (Kac’s principle), and in
[p2] that neighbourhood it is governed by distances of order t. On a closed hyperbolic 2-
[p2] orbifold O a smooth point looks like every other smooth point, because the curvature
[p2] is −1 everywhere. A cone point of order m looks like a disc folded m times around its
[p2] centre: in an orbifold chart the j -th image of a point at distance r from the cone point
[p2] lies at distance about 2r sin(πj/m) (exactly so in the flat approximation; at curvature
[p2] −1, sinh(d/2) = sinh r sin(πj/m)), so   √ the cone point raises the heat kernel on the
[p2] diagonal
[p2]       √    out to a distance of about    t/ sin(π/m), which for large m is much larger
[p2] than t (Figure 1). The trace of the heat kernel adds up these local contributions; it
[p2] has an asymptotic expansion
[p2]                                            X
[p2]                      ZO (t) = tr e−t∆ ∼           cj (O) t j−2   (t ↓ 0),                   (1)
[p2]                                             j≥1
[p2] where ∆ = − div grad is the Laplacian, and its coefficients cj (O), the heat invariants,
[p2] are spectral invariants [1], [2, Thm 4.8]. The j -th invariant is the coefficient of tj−2 .
[p2] Figure 1 shows two orbifolds whose expansions agree in their first two coefficients:
[p2] each orbifold is the double of a triangle, and each panel shows one of its two triangles,
[p2] taken in Poincaré-disc coordinates and lifted to a surface in space (a stylised, not an
[p2] isometric, picture), each point coloured by 4πt ht (x, x) at t = 0.02, where ht is the
[p2] heat kernel of the orbifold, computed on the true hyperbolic triangle as half the sum
[p2] of its Neumann and Dirichlet heat kernels, on one logarithmic scale for both panels.
[p2] This value is near 1 at smooth points and approaches the order at a cone point; it
[p2]                                               2
[p2] 
[p3]                                                     √
[p3] stays well above 1 out to a distance of about t/ sin(π/m) from a cone point of order
[p3] m, about 0.55 for m = 12 (on the flat cone of the same order, 4πt ht is about 1.8 at
[p3] that distance and within 1% of 1 beyond three times it). So the darkest tips are the
[p3] cone points of largest order, two of order 8 in (a) and one of order 12 in (b), though
[p3] at this t and scale their extreme values are confined to a few pixels.
[p3]     This paper asks how many coefficients of this expansion are needed before it dis-
[p3] tinguishes the cone points of one orbifold from those of another, and what decides
[p3] that number. If two orbifolds share c1 , . . . , ck but not ck+1 , the difference of their heat
[p3] traces has exact order tk−1 ; so the least number of heat invariants that determines the
[p3] genus and the cone orders counts the orders in t at which cone points of different orders
[p3] contribute identically to the expansion. It counts coefficients of an asymptotic expan-
[p3] sion, not lengths of time: on any interval of positive times the heat trace determines
[p3] the whole spectrum, and with it every heat invariant, and the companion manuscript
[p3] [3] decides the signature from finitely many eigenvalues through values of the heat
[p3] trace at positive times. Short-time diffusion therefore detects the signature through
[p3] these coefficients only in the limit t → 0: Lemma B.5 and Remark B.6 recover c3 of
[p3] O(2, 8, 8) from values of the heat trace with a proved error, but in our scans only
[p3] from sets of times reaching below 10−3 , with values accurate to a few times 10−8 or
[p3] better, while the computed spectra of this paper fix the trace only for t ≳ 10−3 . The
[p3] counts below are invariants of the sequence of coefficients, not durations of an exper-
[p3] iment. Geometrically, a count measures how many orders of the short-time expansion
[p3] pass before cone points of different orders stop contributing alike; Proposition 2.17
[p3] shows that this is a statement about constant curvature, where the smooth part of
[p3] every invariant is fixed by the area. Two scales limit what the expansion describes. A
[p3] cone point of order m enters through a function with poles at distance π/m from the
[p3] origin (Section 2.2); its coefficients grow like l−1/2 l! (m/π )2l (Lemma B.3), and the
[p3]     (a)                                             (b)
[p3]                            1           2       3       4      6   8     12
[p3]                                            4¼t     t (x; x)
[p3] Fig. 1 One triangle of O(2, 8, 8) (a) and of O(3, 3, 12) (b), stylised, coloured by 4πt ht (x, x) at
[p3] t = 0.02, about 1 at smooth points and rising towards m at a cone point of order m. The two orbifolds
[p3] share their first two heat invariants.
[p3]                                                    3
[p3] 
[p4] expansion describes the heat trace only for t small compared with (π/m)2 . The closed
[p4]                                                                                     2
[p4] geodesics, and with them the shape, enter only through terms of order e−ℓ /4t , ℓ the
[p4] systole, beyond all orders in t, so no heat invariant sees them (Section 4). For large
[p4] orders the first scale is the smaller: for the minimal pair below, (π/12)2 ≈ 0.07 while
[p4] ℓ2 /4 ≈ 0.87, with ℓ the smaller systole, that of O(3, 3, 12). We show that the growth
[p4] of the number is carried exactly by large cone orders. The counts are not stable out-
[p4] side constant curvature: the minimal pair O(2, 8, 8), O(3, 3, 12) of Theorem 1.4 also
[p4] carries metrics that are flat near the cone points and whose heat expansions agree to
[p4] all orders (Section 2.4).
[p4]     Write σ (O) = (g ; m1 , . . . , mn ) for the signature (genus and multiset of cone orders),
[p4] Hk (O) = (c1 , . . . , ck ), Sig for the class of closed orientable hyperbolic 2-orbifolds
[p4] (curvature −1) whose singular points are cone points and Sig0 for its genus-0 part,
[p4] and let Kmult (O; C ) be the least k such that Hk separates O from every member of C
[p4] with a different signature (Definition 2.2). Let
[p4]                                                                                         π
[p4]           f (A) = max{Kmult (O; Sig) : O ∈ Sig, Area(O) ≤ A}                       (A ≥ 21 ),
[p4] and let N (k ) be the least size of a nontrivial solution of degree k of the Prouhet–Tarry–
[p4] Escott problem: two distinct multisets of integers of a common size with equal power
[p4] sums of every exponent 1, . . . , k , with no condition on the power sums of exponent k +1.
[p4] This is the quantity P (k, 2) of [4, §1], whose solutions are non-decreasing sequences, so
[p4] that entries may repeat; the notation N (k ) is that of [5, p. 6], who state the problem
[p4] for sets in their §1. The variant that also requires unequal power sums of exponent k +1
[p4] is W (k, 2) of [4, §1], and N (k ) ≤ W (k, 2). A solution of degree k + 1 is one of degree
[p4] k , so N is nondecreasing; this is used repeatedly below. All logarithms are natural.
[p4] 1.1 Main results
[p4] Theorem 1.1 (Bounded cone orders) Let M ≥ 2.
[p4]  (i) Among orbifolds in Sig whose cone orders are all at most M , the first M heat invariants
[p4]      determine the genus and the cone orders, and the first M − 1 do not.
[p4] (ii) Against all of Sig, the first M + 1 heat invariants determine the genus and the cone
[p4]      orders of an orbifold whose cone orders are at most M , at every area, and M + 1 cannot
[p4]      be lowered uniformly in the area: for every M some pair, of large enough area, shares
[p4]      exactly c1 , . . . , cM ; for M = 2, (0; 2, . . . , 2) with ten cone points and (1; 4, 4, 4, 4), both
[p4]      of area 6π, share exactly c1 , c2 .
[p4] (iii) So the growth in Theorem 1.2 below is carried by large cone orders: an orbifold that
[p4]       needs L + 1 heat invariants, L ≥ 2, has a cone order at least L, and two orbifolds of
[p4]       different signatures that share L heat invariants each have at least ⌈ L−1
[p4]                                                                               2 ⌉ distinct cone
[p4]       orders (Corollary 3.12).
[p4] Theorem 1.2 (Growth and the Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem)
[p4]                           q
[p4]  (i) For A ≥ 8π, f (A) ≥ ⌊ 13 ( 2π
[p4]                                 A
[p4]                                    − 1)⌋ + 2; the elementary upper bound f (A) ≤ ⌊A/π⌋ + 4
[p4]      is Corollary 1.5.
[p4]                                                    4
[p4] 
[p5] (ii) For 0 < α ≤ 1, f (A) ≥ cAα for all large A and some c > 0 if and only if N (k) ≤ Ck1/α
[p5]      for all k ≥ 1 and some C; and f (A) = O(Aα ) if and only if N (k) ≥ ck1/α for all k and
[p5]      some c > 0. In particular f grows linearly if and only if N (k) = O(k), an open problem,
[p5]      and f (A) = o(A) if and only if N (k)/k → ∞.
[p5]     Part (ii) is a reformulation of an open problem, not a solution of it. With the
[p5] known quadratic upper bounds on N (k ), (ii) gives exactly the lower exponent 21 of
[p5] (i); a lower bound f (A) ≥ cAα with α > 12 would prove N (k ) = O(k 2−ϵ ), which is
[p5] not known. The first equivalence has content only for 12 < α ≤ 1, since for α ≤ 21
[p5] both of its sides hold, by (i) and the quadratic bound;√the second has content only for
[p5] 1                           1
[p5] 2 ≤ α ≤ 1, since for α < 2 both of its sides fail. As c A ≤ f (A) ≤ A/π + 4 for large
[p5] A, the open question is not whether f is squeezed between powers of A but which
[p5] power, if any, it follows: whether f (A) is of the order of Aα for some α, and for which.
[p5] Theorem 1.3 (The algebra of a collision)
[p5]  (i) Among hyperbolic spheres with n cone points, the first n heat invariants determine the
[p5]      cone orders. The orders are recovered by solving aQsquare linear system
[p5]                                                                          Q      for their ele-
[p5]      mentary symmetric functions, with determinant ± i<j (mi + mj )/ i mi , the Orlando
[p5]      form of a Hurwitz determinant, and taking the roots of a polynomial. The first n − 1
[p5]      invariants do not suffice for n = 3, 4, already on integer orders, and as functions of
[p5]      real orders they are not injective near any n distinct positive reals; at a non-integral
[p5]      order these functions are an algebraic device (Proposition 2.8), not heat invariants of
[p5]      an orbifold.
[p5] (ii) Changing the genus costs heat invariants: if orbifolds in Sig of genera g ̸= g ′ , with n
[p5]      and n′ cone points and reciprocal sums R, R′ of their orders, share their first L ≥ 2 heat
[p5]      invariants, then n + n′ + |R − R′ | ≥ 2L + 2|g − g ′ |.
[p5] Theorem 1.4 (Triangle orbifolds) Let O(p, q, r) be a hyperbolic triangle orbifold.
[p5]  (i) The first four heat invariants determine its signature among all of Sig (Corollary 5.3);
[p5]      whether three do is equivalent to the absence of solutions of one Diophantine system
[p5]      (Problem 5). Among hyperbolic triangle orbifolds the first three heat invariants determine
[p5]      O(p, q, r) up to isometry, and if p + q + r ≤ 17 the first two do.
[p5] (ii) The orbifolds O(2, 8, 8) and O(3, 3, 12) share their first two heat invariants; among
[p5]      hyperbolic triangle orbifolds they are the only such pair with cone-order sum 18, and no
[p5]      pair has a smaller sum.
[p5] (iii) This pair is isolated among triangle orbifolds: for every integer k ≥ 1 the orbifolds
[p5]       O(2k, 8k, 8k) and O(3k, 3k, 12k) share their first two heat invariants with each other
[p5]       and with no third triangle orbifold. The reason is arithmetic: such collisions are rational
[p5]       points on a plane cubic, which here is an elliptic curve of rank 0 with twelve rational
[p5]       points. For k = 1 the pair is isolated in all of Sig (Theorem 5.10); for k ≥ 2 we claim
[p5]       isolation among triangle orbifolds only.
[p5]    A bound in terms of the area alone follows from the separation argument behind
[p5] these results.
[p5]                                                5
[p5] 
[p6] Corollary 1.5 (A bound from the area) For every O ∈ Sig the first ⌊Area(O)/π⌋ +
[p6] 4 heat invariants determine the genus and the cone orders of O among all of Sig, a
[p6] number computable from c1 = Area /4π. No number independent of the area suffices:
[p6] supO Kmult (O; Sig) = supO Kmult (O; Sig0 ) = ∞.
[p6]    The counts are a statement about constant curvature. With variable curvature the
[p6] smooth part of each heat invariant becomes a new real number at every order, and a
[p6] cone point at which the metric is exactly flat contributes to c2 alone.
[p6] Theorem 1.6 (Variable curvature)
[p6]  (i) At a cone point p of order m ≥ 2 of a Riemannian orbifold, the part of the
[p6]      cone coefficient of tl , l ≥ 1, that is linear in the jet of the curvature K at p is
[p6]                                                    1 Pm−1         πj −2i
[p6]        2
[p6]            Π (m) (−∆)l−1 K(p), with Πi (m) = m
[p6]      (l−1)! l+1                                        j=1 (2 sin m )    (Proposition 2.14).
[p6] (ii) For every L ≥ 2, two signatures whose heat invariant c2 agrees carry metrics, depending
[p6]      on L and of constant curvature near the cone points, with the same first L heat invariants
[p6]      (Proposition 2.17(ii)).
[p6] (iii) O(2, 8, 8) and O(3, 3, 12) carry metrics, flat near the cone points, whose heat invariants
[p6]       agree at every order (Proposition 2.17(iii)).
[p6]     Section 6 concerns the algebraic map that recovers the cone orders of a sphere with
[p6] n cone points from c1 , . . . , cn , extended to real orders, where the cj are an algebraic
[p6] device and not heat invariants of an orbifold. This map is Lipschitz at simple orders
[p6] and Hölder of exponent 1/k at k -fold orders, with constants explicit except for one
[p6] matrix norm, which is itself bounded explicitly (Appendix C); the exponent 1/k cannot
[p6] be improved for arbitrary data, and for data that are the cj of real orders it is 12 at
[p6] a double order and when all n ≥ 3 orders are equal, while other configurations are
[p6] open. Its content for orbifolds is the threshold below which integer orders are recovered
[p6] exactly (Theorem C.4, Proposition C.5, Corollary 6.5). Approximate c1 , . . . , cn can be
[p6] obtained from finitely many values of the heat trace with a proved error (Lemma B.5),
[p6] but for the minimal pair this reaches the threshold only from sets of times reaching
[p6] below 10−3 (Remark B.6), shorter than computed spectra resolve.
[p6]     What is analytic. The analytic results are these. Proposition 2.8, proved in
[p6] Appendix B from the trace formula alone, gives the full small-time expansion of the
[p6] heat trace of a hyperbolic orbifold in closed form, with explicit remainders valid at
[p6] every t > 0: the area series and the cone series have enveloping remainders of opposite
[p6] signs (Proposition B.2, an elementary consequence of the sign of the Taylor remainder
[p6] of e−x ), which give the bound (6). The cone series has the scale (π/m)2 : its coeffi-
[p6] cients grow like l−1/2 l! (m/π )2l (Lemma B.3, Darboux’s method at the nearest poles),
[p6] and truncated at the optimal order it leaves an error exponentially small on that scale
[p6] (Proposition B.4). With a bound for the closed geodesics in terms of the systole and
[p6] the diameter (Lemma 2.5), these give heat invariants from finitely many values of the
[p6] heat trace with a proved error (Lemma B.5). In variable curvature, Theorem 1.6(i)
[p6] holds at every order; beyond order t2 its proof rests on a parametric form of Donnelly’s
[p6] expansion (Lemma E.1) and on the identification of the derivatives of the localised
[p6]                                                6
[p6] 
[p7] fixed-point term with the Duhamel terms of the flat heat trace (Lemma E.2), both
[p7] proved in Appendix E; and Theorem 1.6(ii)–(iii) shows that the counts lose their
[p7] meaning outside constant curvature. Conjecture 2.13, on the full cone term in variable
[p7] curvature, is kept apart from these statements and is used nowhere. Section 4 records
[p7] the classical form of locality, due in substance to Selberg, Huber and McKean, with
[p7] an explicit constant: two orbifolds of one signature have heat traces that differ by at
[p7]                     2
[p7] most Chyp t−1/2 e−ℓ /4t , with exponent and power attained and a constant controlled
[p7] by the diameter (Proposition 4.2); a form without the diameter, through the count
[p7] of closed geodesics of the companion manuscript [3, Lemma 4.5], is taken from there
[p7] (Remark 2.6). So the moduli enter only beyond all orders.
[p7]     What is the algebra of odd power sums. The other input is the triangular structure
[p7] of the heat invariants (Lemma 2.11), which is Uçar’s: each new coefficient adds one
[p7] new odd power sum of the cone orders. From there on the counts are statements
[p7] about odd power sums and the reciprocal sum of a signed multiset of orders, and
[p7] much of it is classical. Corollary 1.5 and the first sentence of Theorem 1.3(i) follow
[p7] from Newton’s identities and Theorem 1.1(i) from a Vandermonde determinant; the
[p7] separation theorem behind them (Theorem 3.7) is the analogue, for odd power sums
[p7] with the reciprocal sum, of the classical bound N (k ) ≥ k + 1, and is proved in the
[p7] same way. The new results on this side are the exact area-free answer M + 1 of
[p7] Theorem 3.11(ii)–(iv), obtained with the classical sign-change property of Chebyshev
[p7] systems [6] (Lemma 3.10) and with witnesses that are divided differences at the nodes
[p7] a2 [7], together with its geometric reading, that the growth of the count is carried by
[p7] large cone orders (Corollary 3.12); the price of a change of genus in Theorem 1.3(ii),
[p7] by Descartes’ rule of signs for polynomials with few nonzero coefficients [8, Part V];
[p7] the closed form of the determinant of the linear system in Theorem 1.3(i), which
[p7] vanishes exactly where a Hurwitz determinant does; the least configurations and their
[p7] relation to the Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem (Lemma A.1, Proposition 3.19); and,
[p7] for triangle orbifolds, Theorem 1.4, whose main geometric statement is the bound of
[p7] four invariants against all of Sig and whose arithmetic part is (iii). Theorem 1.2 is a
[p7] reformulation: it makes linear growth and power lower bounds equivalent to an open
[p7] problem of Diophantine analysis, through the least size of a configuration of signed
[p7] cone orders with vanishing low odd moments (Proposition 3.19), without settling it.
[p7]     Collisions are not isospectral. Orbifolds in Sig with different signatures have dif-
[p7] ferent spectra: by [9, Thm 1.1] the spectrum determines the number of cone points of
[p7] each order, and by [9, Prop. 3.3] the underlying surface, which together give the sig-
[p7] nature; Doyle and Rossetti proved this by a different method [10, Thm 1]. Isospectral
[p7] non-isometric pairs exist in Sig: those of [11, Thm A] have signature (0; 2, 2, 2, 2, 2, 3, 4)
[p7] [11, the sentence following Thm A]; they share a signature and hence every heat invari-
[p7] ant (Section 4). So the collisions below are statements about short-time diffusion, not
[p7] about spectral rigidity.
[p7]     The related manuscripts. Two related manuscripts by the same authors are sub-
[p7] mitted at the same time as this one and are available as preprints: Finitely many
[p7] eigenvalues determine the signature of a hyperbolic orbifold [3], the companion
[p7] manuscript, and Triples with equal sum and equal reciprocal sum [12], the note. The
[p7] companion shows that the first N eigenvalues, each known to accuracy δ , determine
[p7]                                               7
[p7] 
[p8] the signature, with N and δ explicit in the area, a lower bound for the systole and an
[p8] upper bound for the cone orders; it uses results of Sections 2–3 with this paper’s proofs.
[p8] No proof of a result of this paper depends on it; two remarks take inputs from it and
[p8] are marked as such: the count of closed geodesics without a diameter (Remark 2.6) and
[p8] the systole bounds used in Remark B.6. What the two papers share is stated exactly:
[p8] the computed spectra of Section 4 and of the supplement, which the companion uses
[p8] only in the ranges where completeness is checked, the description of the method that
[p8] computed them, and the code and data deposit, which holds the programs of both;
[p8] the companion restates the results of Sections 2 and 3 it uses, with their places here,
[p8] and reproves them in its Appendix A by the arguments of this paper, so that it can
[p8] be read alone; this paper is the primary place of those results and of the description of
[p8] the computations. The counts of heat invariants, their growth and its relation to the
[p8] Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem, the variable-curvature results, the triangle orbifolds
[p8] and the stability of the recovery are in this paper only; the companion adds a diame-
[p8] ter bound, eigenvalue counting and the cusp-like behaviour of high cone orders, which
[p8] are not here. The note studies the arithmetic of the cubic curves that carry collisions
[p8] of triangle orbifolds; Theorem 5.11 is proved here and the note cites it, and no proof
[p8] here depends on the note.
[p8] 1.2 Prior work and what is new
[p8] What is new, in brief. (a) Analytic: the linear part of the cone terms in variable
[p8] curvature at every order (Proposition 2.14, with Lemmas E.1 and E.2) and the exten-
[p8] sion and obstruction results of Proposition 2.17; the optimal truncation of the cone
[p8] series (Proposition B.4, with Lemma B.3) and the proved extraction of heat invariants
[p8] from finitely many values of the heat trace (Lemma B.5); and the explicit constant of
[p8] Proposition 4.2(a). The signed remainders of Proposition B.2 are elementary, and the
[p8] second-variation constants ωn of Proposition 2.17(ii) are re-derived, in the form we
[p8] need, from a classical computation (Remark 2.18). (b) Power sums: the exact answer
[p8] M +1 for bounded orders (Theorem 3.11(ii)–(iv)) and its consequence that the growth
[p8] is carried by large cone orders (Corollary 3.12), the Descartes bound (Theorem 3.15),
[p8] and the reduction of the growth to the Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem (Theorem 3.18,
[p8] Proposition 3.19). (c) Triangle orbifolds and arithmetic: the four invariants against
[p8] all of Sig (Corollary 5.3), the main geometric result of Section 5, the least-area state-
[p8] ment of Theorem 5.10, and the isolation of the minimal pair among triangle orbifolds
[p8] (Theorem 5.11). Theorem 1.2 settles no growth exponent; it makes each exponent
[p8] equivalent to a statement about the Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem.
[p8]     Heat invariants at cone points. The heat expansion of an orbifold is local, with
[p8] a contribution from each singular stratum [1], [2, Thm 4.8]; for a flat cone of angle
[p8] 2π/m the cone coefficient (m2 − 1)/(12m), derived from the Sommerfeld–Carslaw
[p8] kernel on the cone, is classical [13, (4.42)], [14, (6.4)], [15, 16], [17, Rem. 6.6], and for a
[p8] rotationally symmetric cone of arbitrary angle and variable curvature Fursaev derived,
[p8] by a physical argument
[p8]                 P          based on Donnelly’s method, that the n-th cone coefficient
[p8] has the form      k≤n αc k α)Gnk , with ck a polynomial of degree 2k in the inverse
[p8]                           (
[p8] angle and Gnk curvature invariants at the tip, and computed the first two [18, (3.26)–
[p8] (3.28); (2.7)–(2.8) for the first two]; Dowker gave a simpler derivation of Fursaev’s
[p8]                                               8
[p8] 
[p9] second coefficient [19]. At constant curvature Uçar computed every cone coefficient [20,
[p9] Thm 4.20] and recovered the cone orders from the whole sequence of heat invariants
[p9] [20, Cor. 4.21(iv); Cor. 4.23 for the Euler characteristics]; the mechanism, that each
[p9] coefficient adds one new odd power sum with nonzero top coefficient, is the induction of
[p9] his proof for polygons [20, Thm 3.40], and it is our starting point (Lemma 2.11). Other
[p9] singular data heard by heat or by the spectrum: the type of a closed orientable orbifold
[p9] with χ ≥ 0, from c2 alone [2, Thms 5.14–5.15], for isospectral compact orientable
[p9] orbifolds, finiteness of the isotropy types under a lower Ricci curvature bound [21, Main
[p9] Thm 1] and, for isolated singularities under a lower sectional curvature bound, a bound
[p9] on the number of singular points [21, Main Thm 2], finiteness of the diffeomorphism
[p9] types in an isospectral family of 2-orbifolds with a lower curvature bound [22], but not
[p9] always the maximal isotropy order [23]; whether there are singular points at all when
[p9] the singular set has codimension at most 3, through the heat invariants of the 0- and
[p9] 1-spectra of the Hodge Laplacian together [24]; for hyperbolic 2-orbifolds, through the
[p9] trace formula, the number of cone points of each order [9, Thm 1.1], [10]; the weights of
[p9] a weighted projective plane with isolated singularities, from the spectra on functions
[p9] and on 1-forms for a Kähler metric, or from the spectrum on functions and the Euler
[p9] characteristic [25, Thm 1, Thm 6.2]; cone points on surfaces of variable curvature [2],
[p9] [26, Thm 4.1], [27], and curved conic singularities of arbitrary angle with a rotationally
[p9] symmetric metric near the tip, through the first terms of the heat trace [28]; corners
[p9] and cones of arbitrary angle [17, 29, 30]. What is new here is a fixed, finite count
[p9] against the whole class Sig, and its growth: some finite number suffices at each area,
[p9] since only finitely many signatures have a given area (Corollary 3.8); for spheres with a
[p9] known number n of cone points, n invariants suffice by Uçar’s triangular structure and
[p9] Newton’s identities (Theorem 3.2), but to our knowledge no explicit number separating
[p9] an orbifold from all of Sig was known for closed hyperbolic 2-orbifolds. For Euclidean
[p9] triangles the area, the perimeter and the sum of the reciprocals of the angles, which
[p9] are the first three heat invariants, determine the triangle [31, Thm 1], and a triangle
[p9] that shares its first N Dirichlet eigenvalues with a given one, N depending on the first
[p9] two, is isospectral to it [32, Thm 1].
[p9]     Power sums. By Newton’s identities, two n-multisets of complex numbers with
[p9] equal P1 , P3 , . . . , P2n−1 agree after deleting pairs {a, −a} (Lemma 3.1 with the argu-
[p9] ment of Theorem 3.2; odd symmetric systems are studied in [5, §3]), and for n distinct
[p9] positive orders the conclusion of Theorem 3.2 follows from a uniqueness theorem that
[p9] Laurens attributes to Steinig and states right after the statement of his Lemma 3.2:
[p9] any n power sums of n distinct positive real numbers have at most one solution [33,
[p9] after the statement of Lemma 3.2]; see also [34, Prop. 24]. Theorem 3.2 adds com-
[p9] plex multisets with multiplicity, for the system (R, P1 , . . . , P2n−3 ) that heat supplies,
[p9] and Theorem 3.3 the closed form of the determinant; the linear system is the ana-
[p9] logue, with R, of the odd-power-sum Newton identities of [35, §3]. By our search
[p9] (Remark 3.5), the integer witness of Theorem 3.4(3) and the seven other primitive
[p9] witnesses with orders at most 84 are exactly (A.685)–(A.692) of Chen’s survey, which
[p9] lists them as the smallest solutions found by a computer search of 2017 [36, §A.5.8],
[p9] and for integer multisets the forward direction of Theorem 3.4(1) is Chen’s Iden-
[p9] tity 9 with m = 3 [36, (3.27)–(3.28)], stated there for integer sets. The separation
[p9]                                              9
[p9] 
[p10] theorem (Theorem 3.7) is the analogue, for odd power sums with the reciprocal sum,
[p10] of the classical bound N (k ) ≥ k + 1, proved in the same way by Newton’s identities.
[p10] The sign-change argument of Lemma 3.10 is the classical sign-change property of the
[p10] Chebyshev system 1, y, . . . , y L−1 [6], the mechanism of Descartes’ rule; Laurens uses
[p10] it for power sums, in the language of total positivity, in the proofs of his Lemma 3.2
[p10] and Corollary 3.3 [33]. The witnesses of Theorem 3.11(ii) are divided differences at the
[p10] nodes a2 [7], and Theorem 3.15 is proved by Descartes’ rule for polynomials with few
[p10] nonzero coefficients [8, Part V]. New on this side are its use for the exact answer M +1
[p10] (Theorem 3.11(ii)–(iv)), the Descartes bound on the imbalance (Theorem 3.15), dou-
[p10] bling (Proposition 3.16) and the growth equivalence (Theorem 3.18, Proposition 3.19),
[p10] which relocates the growth question to the Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem without
[p10] settling it.
[p10]     Triangle orbifolds. For a hyperbolic triangle orbifold the invariant c of Dryden,
[p10] Gordon, Greenwald and Webb is twelve times the heat invariant c2 [2, (5.13)], so with
[p10] the area it gives exactly the first two heat invariants, and they observe that it “does
[p10] not seem sufficiently strong to distinguish among” the triangular pillows with χ < 0 [2,
[p10] Rem. 5.16]; Theorem 1.4 makes this precise. Two hyperbolic triangle groups with the
[p10] same length spectrum coincide [37], [38, Thm 3.1]. The collisions are rational points
[p10] on the cubics CΛ that Bremner, Guy and Nowakowski study for integer Λ [39, p. 117];
[p10] at Λ = 272 , which they do not treat, the minimal pair is one of their reciprocal pairs.
[p10] Their arithmetic is the subject of the note [12], which determines the torsion of the
[p10] cubic curves that carry them, proves lower bounds for their number and that classes of
[p10] every size occur, and enumerates the collisions up to sum 6000; no proof here depends
[p10] on it. The enumeration of all triads up to sum 4800 in the supplement is the same
[p10] computation as the note’s, restricted to that range. Theorem 5.11 is proved here, by
[p10] hand, because it is the isolation statement of Theorem 1.4(iii), about orbifolds; the
[p10] note cites it.
[p10]     Organisation. Section 2 computes the heat invariants with remainders and shows,
[p10] in Section 2.4, what survives in variable curvature (Theorem 1.6 is Propositions 2.14
[p10] and 2.17, with Lemmas E.1 and E.2 of Appendix E). Section 3 proves Theorems 1.1, 1.2
[p10] and 1.3 (as Theorem 3.11 with Corollary 3.12, Theorem 3.18 with Proposition 3.19, and
[p10] Theorems 3.2–3.4 with Theorem 3.15) and Corollary 1.5 (Corollary 3.8, Theorem 3.17).
[p10] Section 4 shows what the expansion does not see and on which timescales the computed
[p10] traces of the minimal pair differ, Section 5 proves Theorem 1.4, Section 6 the stability
[p10] theorem, and Section 7 lists open problems. Statements resting on computer search
[p10] alone are listed in the supplement (Section S8); none is used in a proof.

[p11] Definition 2.2 (Kiso and Kmult ) For a class C and O ∈ C, Kiso (O; C) and Kmult (O; C) are
[p11] the least k ≥ 1 such that every O′ ∈ C with Hk (O′ ) = Hk (O) is isometric to O, respectively
[p11] has σ(O′ ) = σ(O) (min ∅ = ∞). So Kmult ≤ Kiso .

[p13] Remark 2.6 (A count without the diameter) The proof of Lemma 2.5 uses Lemma 2.4 only
[p13] through nO (x) ≤ Cex with C = πe3 diam / Area(O). The companion manuscript proves, for
[p13] the same counting function, nO (x) ≤ C∗ (A, ε, M ) ex for every O ∈ Sig of area at most A,
[p13] systole at least ε and cone orders at most M , with C∗ explicit [3, Lemma 4.5]. With C∗ in
[p13] place of πe3 diam / Area(O), the bound of Lemma 2.5 and Proposition 4.2(a) hold with the
[p13] same proofs and involve no diameter. This count is taken from the companion manuscript and
[p13] is not proved here; it is used only in this remark and in the comparison after Proposition 4.2,
[p13] and no result of this paper depends on it.

[p15]      The next lemma is the mechanism of Uçar’s proof that the heat invariants of
[p15] a polygon or orbisurface of nonzero constant curvature determine its angles [20,
[p15] Thm 3.40Pand its proof, pp. 98–99]: there the top-degree part of the ν -th angle term
[p15] is Wν = i (π 2ν+2 − γi2ν+2 )/(πγi2ν+1 ) with a coefficient that is a nonzero multiple of
[p15] a Bernoulli number, and “we conclude by induction that the spectrum determines the
[p15] sequence (Wν )”. At the angles γi = π/mi of cone points, Wν = Ψν+1 (m). What is new
[p15] here is the use of this triangular structure with a fixed, finite number of coefficients,
[p15] and the analysis of that number in the rest of the paper.

[p16]     At constant curvature K the expansion holds with K l in place of (−1)l in the cone
[p16] terms and αk (−K )k in place of αk [20, Thm 4.20] (for K < 0 it also follows from
[p16] Proposition 2.8 by scaling, Proposition 2.12, and for K > 0 from Uçar’s theorem, which
[p16] covers every constant curvature); for closed orientable 2-orbifolds with χ ≥ 0, good
[p16] or bad, and every Riemannian metric, the single invariant c2 already determines the
[p16] orbifold type [2, (5.13), Thms 5.14–5.15], and at K = −1 it does not (Theorem 1.4(ii)).
[p16] An unknown K < 0 is one more unknown; the next proposition says what it changes.
[p16] Proposition 2.12 (Unknown constant curvature) Let O be a closed orientable 2-orbifold
[p16] with cone points and a metric g of constant curvature K < 0, and let O1 be the same orbifold
[p16] with the metric |K|g, of curvature −1. Then ZO (t) = ZO1 (|K|t) and cj (O) = |K|j−2 cj (O1 )
[p16] for every j. So c2 does not depend on K, the quantities Ij = cj c1j−2 do not change under
[p16] scaling, and |K| = −χ/(2c1 (O)). Two such orbifolds, of signatures σ, σ ′ , have the same
[p16] c1 , . . . , cL for suitable curvatures K, K ′ < 0 if and only if cj (σ)χ(σ)j−2 = cj (σ ′ )χ(σ ′ )j−2 for
[p16] 2 ≤ j ≤ L, where cj (σ) is computed at curvature −1.
[p16] Proof The Laplacian of |K|g is |K|−1 ∆g , so ZO1 (s) = ZO (s/|K|), and comparing the expan-
[p16] sions (1) gives cj (O) = |K|j−2 cj (O1 ). Hence Ij (O) = cj (O1 )c1 (O1 )j−2 , and c1 (O1 ) =
[p16] Area(O1 )/4π = −χ/2 by Gauss–Bonnet, which gives |K|. If c1 , . . . , cL agree, so do I2 , . . . , IL .
[p16] Conversely, if Ij (σ) = Ij (σ ′ ) for 2 ≤ j ≤ L, choose K, K ′ with c1 (σ)/|K| = c1 (σ ′ )/|K ′ |; then
[p16] c1 agrees, and so does cj = Ij c2−j   1   for 2 ≤ j ≤ L, as c1 ̸= 0. With c1 (σ) = −χ(σ)/2 the
[p16] condition Ij (σ) = Ij (σ ′ ) reads as stated.                                                         □
[p16]     So with an unknown curvature the data are the scale-invariant Ij , and the compar-
[p16] ison is against signatures of every area: signatures of equal χ are compared exactly as
[p16] at curvature −1, and signatures of different χ may now collide. Theorems 1.1 and 1.2
[p16] and Corollary 1.5 are statements at a known curvature, −1 or, by the proposition,
[p16] any known K < 0; they are not claimed for an unknown curvature. The counts also
[p16] concern exact equalities of coefficients and are not stable
[p16]                                                         P under changes of Pthe metric
[p16] outside constant curvature. The invariant c2 = Ω0 + Ri b0 (mi ) = χ/6 + i b0 (mi ) is
[p16]                                                       1
[p16] topological, but c3 is not: its smooth part is Ω1 = 60π    K 2 dA (as u2 = K 2 /15 up to
[p16] a divergence), the cone terms do not change under a change of the metric away from
[p16]                                                    16

[p17] R cone      R and if such a change keeps the area and makes K non-constant,
[p17]          points,                                                                 then

[p17]  1

[p17] as in (5). This per-point statement is not Proposition 2.8, which gives only the sum
[p17] of the cone terms over the cone points: it uses Donnelly’s locality [2, Thm 4.8] and
[p17] Uçar’s computation of the contribution of each cone point [20, Thm 4.20], and so does
[p17] this section wherever it uses the value of bl (p) at constant curvature. The claim of
[p17] Appendix B that no result rests on the locality theorem concerns Sections 3–6. The
[p17] mechanism behind the main result of this section is simple: at a cone point where
[p17] the metric is exactly flat, every cone term bl (p) with l ≥ 1 vanishes, so a flat cone
[p17] point contributes to c2 alone, and the smooth parts Ωl , which carry no structure,
[p17] decide everything else. Two things change, and they have different effects. Throughout,
[p17] ∆ = − div grad as in (1), C = 2 sin ϑ2 , [v n ]F is the coefficient of v n in a power series F ,

[p17] where the isotropy acts by rotations.
[p17]     The cone term. For orders m ≥ l − 1, one new odd power of the order should still
[p17] enter at order tl (Conjecture 2.13), but its coefficient βl,l+1 (p) is no longer a power
[p17] of K (p), and it vanishes, for instance, near a flat cone point. In dimension 2 the
[p17] metric in normal coordinates is, to every finite order, a universal polynomial in the
[p17] jet of K at the centre; the jet below means the jet of K , and ∇j K has weight j + 2.
[p17] Here and below ∆ = − div grad is the non-negative Laplace–Beltrami operator of the
[p17] metric itself, the convention of Schueth [26, §1] as well, so that her coefficients are
[p17] quoted with their signs; (−∆)l−1 K is the iterated Laplacian with the sign of ∂x2 + ∂y2 ;
[p17] computed in the metric, ∆2 K (p) differs from the corresponding expression of the
[p17] flat chart in normal coordinates by terms of degree 2 in the jet, and the coefficients
[p17] of Remark 2.16 refer to this convention. The radial part of the jet at p is the jet
[p17] of K̄ , the jet of the curvature of the rotationally symmetric metric dr2 + f (r)2 dθ2
[p17] below. Section 3 is used only in Proposition 2.17, through Lemma 3.6, and only for
[p17] its consequences; Lemma 3.6 uses only Lemma 2.11, so there is no circularity. For
[p17] a rotationally symmetric cone of arbitrary angle α, Fursaev derived, by a physical
[p17]                                               17

[p18]                                                                        P
[p18] argument based on Donnelly’s method, the n-th cone coefficient as k≤n αck (α)Gnk ,
[p18] with ck a polynomial of degree 2k in the inverse angle and Gnk curvature invariants
[p18] at the tip, and computed the first two [18, (3.26)–(3.28); (2.7)–(2.8) for the first two];
[p18] for surfaces with curved conic singularities of arbitrary angle, rotationally symmetric
[p18] near the tip, Suleymanova expresses the first terms of the heat trace through the angle
[p18] and the curvature at the tip [28], and Schueth computes the coefficient of t2 at orbifold
[p18] cone points [26, Thm 4.1]. Conjecture 2.13(i) below is this structure for an orbifold
[p18] cone point whose germ need not be rotationally symmetric, and (ii) identifies its top
[p18] coefficient. Part of it is proved: the part linear in the jet (Proposition 2.14) and the
[p18] value at constant curvature ([20, Thm 4.20] with [2, Thm 4.8], as above). What is
[p18] conjectured is (i) for germs that are not rotationally symmetric, (i) for rotationally
[p18] symmetric germs beyond Fursaev’s derivation, and the part of βl,l+1 of degree at
[p18] least 2 in the jet. A derivation in outline is given in the supplement (Section S7). It
[p18] uses Donnelly’s theorem that the fixed-point coefficient depends on dΦp only through
[p18] (I − dΦp )−1 , as stated in [2, 4.2]; two of its steps are not carried out in full: the
[p18] identification of the radial part of the jet with the jet of dr2 + f (r)2 dθ2 up to terms
[p18] at least quadratic in the non-radial modes, and the uniformity in ϑ of the inversion
[p18] s 7→ r and of Laplace’s method. Its formulas are confirmed by computation for l ≤ 3
[p18] on rotationally symmetric germs (Remark 2.16). The conjecture is kept apart from the
[p18] proved statements of this section: no result of the paper uses it, and Propositions 2.14
[p18] and 2.17 do not depend on it.

[p18] Proposition 2.14 (The linear part of the cone term) Let p be a cone point of order m ≥ 2
[p18] of a Riemannian orbifold. For every l ≥ 1 the part of bl (p) that is linear in the jet of K at
[p18] p is λl (m) (−∆)l−1 K(p) for a number λl (m), and λl (m) = (l−1)!
[p18]                                                                 2
[p18]                                                                      Πl+1 (m). For l ≤ 2 this
[p18] value is also the K term of [2, (4.4)] for l = 1 and the ∆K term of Schueth’s t2 coefficient
[p18] [26, Thm 4.1] for l = 2.
[p18] Proof Let Φ be a local isometry fixing p, with dΦp the rotation by ϑ ∈ (0, 2π). By Donnelly’s
[p18] theorem [44], [2, 4.1, 4.2, Thm 4.8], Bl (Φ) is a universal polynomial in the jet at p whose
[p18] coefficients depend on ϑ, given by an expression that does not change when the orthonormal
[p18] frame at p is rotated; rotating the frame by ρ ∈ SO(2) replaces the jet j by ρ · j and leaves
[p18]                                                  18
[p18] 
[p19] dΦp unchanged, so Bl (Φ) is an SO(2)-invariant polynomial in the jet. It has weight 2l, and
[p19] ∇j K has weight j + 2, so its part linear in the jet is a linear form in ∇2l−2 K(p) alone. By
[p19] the Ricci identities, ∇2l−2 K(p) is symmetric up to terms of degree at least 2 in the jet, since
[p19] every commutator of covariant derivatives brings in a factor of the curvature; so the linear
[p19] part is an SO(2)-invariant linear form on the symmetric tensors of rank 2l − 2 on R2 . As
[p19] a representation of SO(2) these split into the frequencies 2l − 2, 2l − 4, . . . , 0, each once, so
[p19] the invariant linear forms are the multiples of the full trace, which is (−∆)l−1 K(p); hence
[p19] the linear part is λl (ϑ)(−∆)l−1 K(p). To find λl , take the metric gε = e2εψ |dx|2 on R2 with
[p19] ψ = ζ0 |x|2l , where ζ0 ∈ Cc∞ (R2 ) is radial and equal to 1 near 0; the rotation Rotϑ is an
[p19] isometry, and the fixed-point term at 0 depends only on the germ there, where ψ = |x|2l .

[p20]     The smooth term. At constant curvature Ωl is fixed by c1 . In general Ωl is a new
[p20] real number at every order, and it decides everything.
[p20] Proposition 2.17 (Extension and obstruction) Let O, O′ be closed orientable orbifolds
[p20] whose singular points are cone points, with Riemannian metrics.
[p20]  (i) Let K0 ̸= 0 and L ≥ 2. If at every cone point of both, K = K0 and ∇j K = 0 for
[p20]      1 ≤ j ≤ 2L − 6, and Ωl (O) = Ωl (O′ ) for −1 ≤ l ≤ L − 2, then HL (O) = HL (O′ ) if
[p20]      and only if (13) holds. Here χ(O) = χ(O′ ) is part of the hypothesis, as Ω0 = χ/6. For
[p20]      K0 = 0 and L ≥ 3 the direction from HL (O) = HL (O′ ) to (13) fails.
[p20] (ii) Let L ≥ 2 and K0 ∈ R. The signatures (g; m) and (g ′ ; m′ ) carry metrics of constant cur-
[p20]                                                                                     P (mi −1)2
[p20]      vature K0 near their cone points with the same HL if and only if 2−2g    6 +      i 12mi =

[p20]                         j
[p20]       χ = 0: in variable curvature c2 does not determine χ.
[p20] (iii) If i (1 − m1i ) = j (1 − m1′ ) > 1 and i (mi − m1i ) = j (m′j − m1′ ), then for every

[p22] Remark 2.18 (The constants ωn and the leading terms of Branson, Gilkey and Ørsted)
[p22] The constants ωn are the coefficients of the part quadratic in the curvature, with the most
[p22] derivatives, of the integrated heat invariants. Branson, Gilkey and Ørsted computed the
[p22] heat invariants of every order modulo cubic terms, for operators of Laplace type in every
[p22] dimension, by deriving their quadratic terms from their linear ones [47]; in physics the same
[p22] quantities are the form factors of covariant perturbation theory, built from the moments
[p22] R1           n
[p22]  0 (s(1 − s)) ds [48, §8.2]. The proof above re-derives the two-dimensional, conformally flat
[p22] case in the form we need, and the mechanism of [47] gives the same constants. With ∂θ ∆θ =

[p23]     Constant curvature is therefore the setting in which the counts of this paper are
[p23] about the cone points: there the smooth part of every invariant is a known multiple of
[p23] the area, and the triangular structure of Lemma 2.11 is all that remains. With vari-
[p23] able curvature the cone orders should still enter through at most one new odd power
[p23] per order, for large enough orders, as Conjecture 2.13 and Proposition 2.17(i) indicate,
[p23] but they are entangled with the smooth parts, which carry no structure: for each L,
[p23] two signatures carry metrics, depending on L, with the same first L heat invariants
[p23] whenever their c2 agree (Proposition 2.17(ii)), and pairs such as O(2, 8, 8), O(3, 3, 12)
[p23] carry metrics whose heat invariants agree at every order (Proposition 2.17(iii); Prob-
[p23] lem 6), because flat cone points contribute only to c2 , which is then fixed by χ and

[p23] 3 Hearing the signature
[p23] Comparing two orbifolds means studying one signed multiset, the cone orders of the
[p23] first together with the negatives of those of the second, whose reciprocal sum and
[p23] low odd moments vanish. Most of what follows comes from one fact: such a multiset,
[p23] if small compared with the number of vanishing moments, is symmetric under x 7→
[p23] −x; the bounds on the cone orders and on the genus rest instead on sign changes
[p23] (Lemma 3.10, Theorem 3.15).

[p24] Theorem 3.2 (Audibility of the cone orders) Let m be a multiset of n ≥ 1 nonzero complex
[p24] numbers with mi + mj ̸= 0 for all i < j, equivalently en Hurn−1 (χm ) ̸= 0. If m′ is a multiset
[p24] of n nonzero complex numbers with In (m′ ) = In (m), then m′ = m. In particular the first n

[p24] all such spheres: Kmult (O; Sig0,n ) ≤ n.

[p24] then −x ∈/ AP by hypothesis, so multm′ (x) = multX (−x) = multX (x) ≥ multm (x); summing

[p25] Theorem 3.4 (The fibres of n − 1 invariants)
[p25]  (1) Pair criterion (an odd symmetric system in the sense of [5, §3]; the forward direction is
[p25]      [36, Identity
[p25]               Q 9 with m  Q= 3]). For multisets m, m′ of n ≥ 2 nonzero complex numbers and
[p25]      Q(z) = i (z − mi ) j (z + mj ), In−1 (m) = In−1 (m′ ) if and only if Q(z) − Q(−z) =
[p25]                                    ′
[p25]      2κz 3 for some κ ∈ C; if mi + mj ̸= 0 for i < j, then κ = 0 exactly when m′ = m.
[p25]  (2) Real sharpness. For n ≥ 2, every multiset of n distinct positive reals lies on a real curve
[p25]      of multisets of positive reals sharing In−1 .
[p25]  (3) Integer sharpness for n = 3, 4. {2, 8, 8} and {3, 3, 12} share I2 (R = 34 , P1 = 18) and
[p25]                                                                                   8
[p25]      have P3 = 1032 ̸= 1782; {3, 10, 15, 30} and {4, 5, 21, 28} share I3 (R = 15    , P1 = 58,
[p25]      P3 = 31402) and have P5 = 25159618 ̸= 21298618; this pair is [36, (A.685)]. So
[p25]      Kmult (O; Sig0,n ) = n for these orbifolds.
[p25] Proof (1) As in Theorem 3.2, agreement gives ej (X) = 0 for odd j ̸= 2n−3, so Q(z)−Q(−z) =
[p25] −2e2n−3 (X)z 3 , and conversely by the second half of Lemma 3.1; if κ = 0, Q is even and
[p25] m′ = m. (2) Scaling column i of the (n − 1) × n Jacobian of In−1 by m2i gives the matrix
[p25] with rows ϖr m2ri , 0 ≤ r ≤ n − 2 (ϖ0 = −1, ϖr = 2r − 1), whose rows are, up to these
[p25] nonzero factors, those of a Vandermonde matrix in the distinct m2i ; it has rank n − 1, so the
[p25] fibre is a smooth curve near m, whose other points are multisets different from m. (3) Direct
[p25] computation; the four multisets are hyperbolic signatures, and spheres with n cone points
[p25] share Hn−1 exactly when they share In−1 .                                                   □
[p25]                                                       25

[p26] Remark 3.5 (Integer witnesses) A witness for n is a pair of distinct n-multisets of integers
[p26] at least 2 with equal In−1 ; it is disjoint by Theorem 3.2, and rational witnesses scale to
[p26] integer ones. For n = 4 an exhaustive exact search of all 4-multisets with orders at most 440
[p26] finds 193 witnesses, 107 of them primitive (entries with greatest common divisor 1), and no
[p26] three multisets with a common (R, P1 , P3 ); the eight primitive ones with all orders at most 84
[p26] are those of [36, (A.685)–(A.692)], and 101 of the 107 admit a pencil splitting, a construction
[p26] described in the supplement (Section S1). For n = 5 no witness has all orders at most 120,
[p26] which is weak evidence either way. The supplement (Section S1) gives the details.
[p26] 3.2 Heat data of a signature
[p26] Lemma 3.6 (Heat data of a signature) Let O, O′ ∈ Sig have signatures (g; m) and (g ′ ; m′ )
[p26] with n and n′ cone points, and L ≥ 1. Then HL (O) = HL (O′ ) if and only if
[p26]      2g + n − R(m) = 2g ′ + n′ − R(m′ )        and      Ψk (m) = Ψk (m′ ) (1 ≤ k ≤ L − 1).          (13)
[p26]                 ′                 ′            ′                                              max(d,0)
[p26] Then d = R(m ) − R(m) = 2(g − g) + n − n ∈ Z, and the paddings U = m ⊎ {1}                               ,
[p26] V = m′ ⊎ {1}max(−d,0) satisfy
[p26]   R(U ) = R(V ),        Pj (U ) = Pj (V ) (j odd, j ≤ 2L − 3),         |V | − |U | = 2(g − g ′ ),   (14)
[p26]                                   ′   ′    ′
[p26] and |U | + |V | = 2 max(n + g − g , n + g − g). Conversely, if paddings by 1s satisfy (14), the
[p26] two orbifolds have equal area and equal HL .
[p26] Proof c1 agrees exactly when the areas do, the first equation; then c2 , . . . , cL agree exactly
[p26] when C0 , . . . , CL−2 do (Lemma 2.11). An order 1 changes neither n − R nor any Ψk , and the
[p26] rest is direct computation, with Area(g; m) = 2π(2g − 2 + |U | − R(U )).                       □
[p26] Theorem 3.7 (Separation) Let O, O′ ∈ Sig have different signatures and equal HL , and
[p26] let U ∗ , V ∗ be the paddings of Lemma 3.6 with their common elements removed (they do not
[p26] depend on the paddings). Then |U ∗ | + |V ∗ | ≥ 2L + 2. In particular, if L ≥ max(n + g − g ′ , n′ +
[p26] g ′ − g), equal HL implies equal signatures.
[p26] Proof The reduced multisets. Two admissible paddings differ by the same number of entries
[p26] 1 on both sides, since |V | − |U | = 2(g − g ′ ) is fixed; so U ∗ , V ∗ do not depend on the choice.
[p26] Cancelling an element common to both sides preserves (14), so U ∗ and V ∗ are disjoint, satisfy
[p26] (14), and |V ∗ | − |U ∗ | = 2(g − g ′ ). Hence κ = |U ∗ | + |V ∗ | is even. If κ = 0 then m = m′ (the
[p26] entries 1 aside) and g = g ′ , so different signatures have κ ≥ 2.
[p26]     A short configuration is symmetric. Suppose 2 ≤ κ ≤ 2L, and let X ∗ = U ∗ ⊎ (−V ∗ ), a
[p26] multiset of κ nonzero numbers. For odd j, sj (X ∗ ) = Pj (U ∗ ) − Pj (V ∗ ), which vanishes for
[p26] j ≤ 2L − 3 by (14), hence for j ≤ κ − 3; by Lemma 3.1, ej (X ∗ ) = 0 for odd j ≤ κ − 3.
[p26] The only remaining odd index below κ is κ − 1, and eκ−1 (X ∗ ) = eκ (X ∗ ) x∈X ∗ x−1 =
[p26]                                                                                         P
[p26] eκ (X ∗ ) R(U ∗ ) − R(V ∗ ) = 0. So Q(z) = x∈X ∗ (z − x) = j (−1)j ej (X ∗ )z κ−j has only
[p26]                                                 Q                      P
[p26] even powers of z, Q(−z) = Q(z), and X ∗ = −X ∗ with multiplicities. For x > 0 this reads
[p26] multU ∗ (x) = multV ∗ (x), so U ∗ = V ∗ , and disjointness gives U ∗ = V ∗ = ∅, against κ ≥ 2.
[p26]     Conclusion. So different signatures have κ ≥ 2L + 1, and as κ is even, κ ≥ 2L + 2. Finally
[p26] κ ≤ |U | + |V | = 2 max(n + g − g ′ , n′ + g ′ − g), so if L is at least this maximum, equal HL
[p26] forces equal signatures.                                                                           □

[p27] Corollary 3.8 (Genus and cone orders from ⌊Area /π⌋ + 4 heat invariants) For every O ∈
[p27] Sig, Kmult (O; Sig) ≤ ⌊Area(O)/π⌋ + 4. A hyperbolic sphere of area A has at most A/π + 4
[p27] cone points, with equality exactly when all orders are 2; so the bound needs only the area,
[p27] which c1 gives, and not the number of cone points.
[p27] Proof Each cone point adds at least 21 to
[p27]                                              P
[p27]                                                 i (1 − 1/mi ), so n + 4g ≤ Area
[p27]                                                                              P /π + 4, with
[p27] equality in genus 0 exactly when all orders P are 2. With n and g bounded, i 1/mi = n +
[p27] 2g − 2 − Area /2π is fixed, and an equation i≤n 1/mi = r has only finitely manyP     solutions
[p27] in integers 2 ≤ m1 ≤ · · · ≤ mn , since mk ≤ (n − k + 1)/rk with rk = r − i<k 1/mi ; so
[p27] only finitely many signatures have a given area. The same holds for O′ of the same area, so
[p27] n + g − g ′ ≤ n + 4g ≤ Area /π + 4, likewise for n′ + g ′ − g, and Theorem 3.7 applies.     □

[p28] 3.3 Bounded cone orders
[p28] A bound on the cone orders does better than Corollary 3.8: it bounds the number of
[p28] heat invariants independently of the area, and it locates the growth of Section 3.4.
[p28] For an integer M ≥ 1 let Sig≤M be the class of O ∈ Sig whose cone orders are all at
[p28] most M (Sig≤1 consists of the surfaces), and let dO be the number of distinct cone
[p28] orders of O. The upper bound against all of Sig rests on the following classical sign-
[p28] change argument, the mechanism behind Descartes’ rule of signs; Laurens gives the
[p28] same argument for power sums, in the language of total positivity [33, Lemma 3.2,
[p28] Cor. 3.3].
[p28] Lemma 3.10 (Sign changes) Let ν = ri=1 wi δyi with y1 < · · · < yr real, r ≥ 1 and all
[p28]                                        P
[p28] wi ̸= 0, and suppose i wi yik = 0 for 0 ≤ k ≤ L − 1. Then the sequence w1 , . . . , wr has at
[p28]                       P
[p28] least L sign changes.
[p28] Proof Suppose it has S ≤ L − 1 sign changes, between yis and yis +1 for 1 ≤ s ≤ S. The
[p28] polynomial q(y) = ± S           1

[p28]                                                                            P sign exactly
[p28] at these midpoints, so with the right sign wi q(yi ) > 0 for every i. Then  i wi q(yi ) > 0,
[p28] although it vanishes because q is a combination of 1, y, . . . , y L−1 .                  □

[p29]                                                                                  P
[p29] elements counted with multiplicity. As U ∗ and V ∗ are disjoint, every point of its support
[p29] carries a nonzero weight, positive on U ∗ and negative on V ∗ , and x 7→ x2 preserves the order
[p29] of positive numbers. Its moments are R(U ∗ ) − R(V ∗ ) for k = 0 and P2k−1 (U ∗ ) − P2k−1 (V ∗ )
[p29] for k ≥ 1, and by (14), which survives the removal of common elements, they vanish for 0 ≤
[p29] k ≤ L − 1. So the weights, read from left to right, change sign at least L times (Lemma 3.10).
[p29] The positive points are squares of cone orders of O or of 1, so they lie in [1, M 2 ], and every
[p29] point above M 2 is negative. The points in [1, M 2 ] are among the M squares 1, 4, . . . , M 2 , so
[p29] they give at most M − 1 sign changes among themselves, and the negative points above M 2
[p29] add at most one: L ≤ M . Next, the positive points take at most dO + 1 values, the extra one
[p29]                                                 29
[p29] 
[p30] being 1, the least possible point. Each maximal run of positive points causes at most two sign
[p30] changes, and a run containing 1 lies at the left end and causes at most one, so L ≤ 2dO + 1;
[p30] symmetrically L ≤ 2dO′ + 1. The bound on Kmult follows, with Corollary    P3.8.

[p30] are positive, so ν(X) < 0 and X ∈ m′ . Hence Kmult (O; Sig) ≥ M + 1, and (iii) gives
[p30] equality.                                                                                         □
[p30] Corollary 3.12 (The growth needs large cone orders)
[p30]  (a) If two orbifolds in Sig with different signatures share c1 , . . . , cL , L ≥ 2, each has a cone
[p30]      order at least L and at least ⌈ L−12 ⌉ distinct cone orders, and one of them has a cone
[p30]      order at least L + 1.
[p30]  (b) If L ≥ 2 and Kmult (O; Sig) ≥ L + 1, then O has a cone order at least L.
[p30]  (c) fM (A) = max{Kmult (O; Sig) : O ∈ Sig≤M , Area(O) ≤ A} satisfies fM (A) ≤ M + 1
[p30]      for every A, with equality once A is at least the area of a pair of (iv).
[p30] Proof (a) Part (iii), with M the largest cone order of each orbifold, gives L ≤ M and
[p30] L ≤ 2d + 1; for a surface, dO = 0, and (iii) gives L ≤ 1, so it shares at most c1 with an
[p30] orbifold of another signature. If all orders of both were at most L, part (i) with L for M
[p30] would give equal signatures. (b) is (a), and (c) is (iii) and (iv).                      □
[p30] 3.4 How many invariants: the Prouhet–Tarry–Escott
[p30]     connection
[p30] Definition 3.13 (Configurations) An L-configuration is a P     nonempty finite multiset Z of
[p30] nonzero rationals of even size, with no pair {z, −z}, such that z∈Z z j = 0 for odd j ≤ 2L−3
[p30] and z∈Z z −1 = 0. Its imbalance is ι(Z) = #{z > 0} − #{z < 0}.
[p30]     P
[p30] Lemma 3.14 (Configurations and pairs) Let L ≥ 2.
[p30]  (a) Every L-configuration has |Z| ≥ 2L + 2.
[p30]  (b) If O, O′ ∈ Sig have different signatures (g; m), (g ′ ; m′ ) and share c1 , . . . , cL , then, with
[p30]      U ∗ , V ∗ as in Theorem 3.7, Z = U ∗ ⊎ (−V ∗ ) is an L-configuration with ι(Z) = 2(g ′ − g)
[p30]      and |Z| ≤ 2⌊Area(O)/π⌋ + 8.
[p30]  (c) Conversely, let Z be an L-configuration scaled by a positive rational to integers with
[p30]      greatest common divisor 1, and put U = {z ∈ Z : z > 0}, V = {−z : z ∈ Z, z < 0}.
[p30]      For integers g ≥ 0 and g ′ = g + ι(Z)/2 ≥ 0 the signatures (g; U \ {1}) and (g ′ ; V \ {1}),
[p30]      every entry 1 removed, are different and have the same area 2π(2g − 2 + |U | − R(U )).
[p30]      If this area is P
[p30]                      positive, the two orbifolds share c1 , . . . , cL , and they share exactly these
[p30]      if and only if z∈Z z 2L−1 ̸= 0. If the smaller of the two genera is the least that makes
[p30]      the area positive, the area is less than 2π(|W | − 2) when that genus is 0, W being U if
[p30]      g ≤ g ′ and V otherwise, and at most 4π otherwise.
[p30]                                                   30
[p30] 
[p31] Proof (a) The proof of Theorem 3.7 uses only these conditions on X ∗ , except at its last
[p31] step, where for a configuration Newton’s identities force Z = −Z, which the absence of
[p31] pairs {z, −z} excludes; so |Z| ≥ 2L + 1, and |Z| is even. (b) By the proof of Theorem 3.7,
[p31]   ∗
[p31] U
[p31] P and     V ∗ are disjoint, so Z has no pair {z, −z}, and P Z −1̸= ∅; |Z| = |U ∗ | + |V ∗ | is even,
[p31]                   ∗          ∗
[p31]    Z z j
[p31]          = P j (U   )−P j (V   ) = 0 for odd j ≤ 2L−3 and  Z z     = R(U ∗ )−R(V ∗ ) = 0 by (14),
[p31] and ι(Z) = |U | − |V | = 2(g − g). Finally |Z| ≤ |U | + |V | = 2 max(n + g − g ′ , n′ + g ′ − g) ≤
[p31]                  ∗       ∗         ′
[p31] 2⌊Area /π⌋ + 8 (proof of Corollary 3.8). (c) The conditions on Z are homogeneous, so they
[p31] survive the scaling. U and V are disjoint, sinceP Z has no pair {z, −z}, and |U | − |V | = ι(Z) is
[p31] even, as |Z| is. An order 1 adds nothing to (1−1/m), so the areas are 2π(2g−2+|U |−R(U ))
[p31] and 2π(2g ′ − 2 + |V | − R(V )), equal because 2g + |U | = 2g ′ + |V | and R(U ) = R(V ). If
[p31] U \ {1} = V \ {1}, disjointness makes both empty, so U, V ⊆ {1}, one of them is empty and
[p31] the reciprocal sum of Z is ±|Z| ̸= 0; so the signatures differ. With U, V as paddings, (14)
[p31] holds, so by Lemma 3.6 the two orbifolds share c1 , . . . , cL ; as R and the lower odd power
[p31] sums agree, cL+1 differs exactly when P2L−1 (U ) ̸= P2L−1 (V ) (Lemma 2.11). If the smaller
[p31] genus is 0, the area is 2π(|W | − 2 − R(W )) < 2π(|W | − 2); if it is positive and least, lowering
[p31] both genera by 1 makes the area nonpositive, so the area is at most 4π.                           □
[p31]     So pairs of orbifolds sharing their first L heat invariants and L-configurations
[p31] correspond to each other (Lemma 3.14). Since Z has no pair {z, −z}, Z and −Z form
[p31] a solution of degree 2L − 2 of the Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem, so |Z| ≥ N (2L − 2).
[p31] Theorem 3.15 (Descartes bound) Let L ≥ 2, as in Lemma 3.14. Every L-configuration has
[p31] |ι(Z)| ≤ |Z| − 2L. Hence if orbifolds of genera g ̸= g ′ share their first L heat invariants, then
[p31] |U ∗ | + |V ∗ | ≥ 2L + 2|g − g ′ |; and if g ̸= g ′ and the configuration has the least size allowed
[p31] by Theorem 3.7, |Z| = |U ∗ | + |V ∗ | = 2L + 2, where U ∗ , V ∗ are formed from the paddings of
[p31] Lemma 3.6, so that entries 1 count, then |ι(Z)| = 2, |g−g ′ | = 1 and {|U ∗ |, |V ∗ |} = {L, L+2}.
[p31] (If g = g ′ , then ι(Z) = 0.)
[p31] Proof Let κ = |Z| and Q(x) = z∈Z (x − z) = k (−1)k ek xκ−k . By Lemma 3.1, ek = 0 for
[p31]                                   Q                P
[p31]                                   P −1
[p31] odd k ≤ 2L − 3, and eκ−1 = eκ z z          = 0, so at most (κ − 2L)/2 coefficients of odd degree
[p31] are nonzero. Let V ± be the numbers of sign changes of Q(x) and Q(−x). Consecutive nonzero
[p31] coefficients of degrees d > d′ contribute to V + +V − at most d−d′ , and to V + −V − zero if d−d′
[p31] is even and ±1 if it is odd; so V + + V − ≤ κ. All roots are real and nonzero, so by Descartes’
[p31] rule #{z > 0} ≤ V + , #{z < 0} ≤ V − with total κ; both are equalities and ι = V + − V − . A
[p31] gap between consecutive nonzero coefficients whose degrees have different parity has exactly
[p31] one endpoint of odd degree, and each nonzero coefficient of odd degree is an endpoint of at
[p31] most two gaps; so there are at most κ − 2L such gaps, and |ι| = |V + − V − | ≤ κ − 2L. Finally
[p31] ι = 2(g ′ − g).                                                                                □
[p31] Proposition 3.16 (Doubling) Let L ≥ 2, and let X ̸= Y be n-multisets of positive integers
[p31] with Pj (X) = Pj (Y ) for odd j ≤ 2L − 3, U = X ⊎ 2Y ⊎ 2Y , V = Y ⊎ 2X ⊎ 2X, and
[p31] multX , multY the multiplicities of 1 in X, Y . Then (0; U \ {1}) and (0; V \ {1}), every 1
[p31] removed, are hyperbolic, with distinct signatures, equal area < 2π(3n − 2), equal first L heat
[p31] invariants, and 3n − multX and 3n − multY cone points.
[p31]                                                 31
[p31] 
[p32] Proof For every j, including j = −1, Pj (U ) − Pj (V ) = (1 − 2j+1 )(Pj (X) − Pj (Y )), and
[p32] |U | = |V | = 3n; so Lemma 3.6, with U, V as paddings, gives equal area 2π(|U | − R(U ) − 2) <
[p32] 2π(3n − 2) and equal HL , once both are hyperbolic. Here n ≥ 2, since distinct multisets
[p32] P equal P1 have at least two elements; V contains the 2n entries of 2X ⊎ 2X, all ≥ 2, so
[p32] with
[p32]    (1 − 1/v) ≥ n > 2 for n ≥ 3, and for n = 2, Y has at most one 1, so V has five entries
[p32] ≥ 2; likewise for U . If ν(x) is the multiplicity of x in X minus that in Y and x∗ the largest
[p32] x with ν(x) ̸= 0, then 2x∗ has multiplicity −2ν(x∗ ) ̸= 0 in U minus V .                     □
[p32] Theorem 3.17 (No uniform number of heat invariants) For every L ≥ 2 there are orbifolds
[p32] in Sig of different genera, whose smaller genus can be made equal to any prescribed g ≥ 2, and
[p32] orbifolds in Sig0 with different numbers of cone points, sharing their first L heat invariants.
[p32] Hence supO Kmult (O; Sig) = supO Kmult (O; Sig0 ) = ∞.
[p32] Proof The genus pairs are built in the proof of Theorem 3.18(c), with smaller genus at most
[p32] 2; adding equally many handles to both keeps the areas, hence the invariants, equal. For the
[p32] cone count, take a nontrivial solution of degree 2L − 3 of the Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem
[p32] [5, Prop. 3], cancel common elements and translate so that the least element is 1, in X say;
[p32] Proposition 3.16 with multX ≥ 1, multY = 0 applies.                                       □
[p32]     For the growth, let fg (A) and fn (A) be the maxima over orbifolds of area at
[p32] most A of the least k such that every O′ ∈ Sig (respectively every genus-0 O′ ) with
[p32] Hk (O′ ) = Hk (O) has the same genus (respectively the same number of cone points),
[p32] fg being maximised over all O ∈ Sig and fn over genus-0 O; fg , fn ≤ f . One has
[p32] k + 1 ≤ N (k ) ≤ 21 k (k + 1) + 1 [4, §1], the upper bound by the pigeonhole principle [5,
[p32] Props. 2–3], which gives P (k, m) ≤ 12 k (k +1)+1 for every number m of systems [4, §1].
[p32] In the notation of Croot, Mao and Yip [4, §1], N (k ) = P (k, 2), while W (k, m) asks in
[p32] addition that the power sums of exponent k +1 differ; the pigeonhole principle does not
[p32] readily bound W (k, 2). Wright introduced this variant [50]; Hua [51, 52] and Wright
[p32] gave upper bounds on W (k, m) by elementary arguments, and the best known bound,
[p32] W (k, m) ≤ 12 k (k +1)+1, is due to Wooley, all as recalled in [4, §1]. As N (k ) ≤ W (k, 2),
[p32] every upper bound on W (k, 2) bounds N (k ); Melzak gave a slightly stronger quadratic
[p32] bound for N (k ), as reported in [5, p. 7]. All these bounds are quadratic in k . The
[p32] statements below about N (k ) are about P (k, 2), and N is nondecreasing (Section 1).
[p32] To our knowledge no bound o(k 2 ) is known: a bound N (k ) = o(k 2 ) is Problem 3 of [5,
[p32] §6], and the authors remark there that no progress on this problem and the next has
[p32] been made for many years; we know of no later bound o(k 2 ), the bounds recalled in
[p32] the 2026 preprint [4, §1] being the quadratic ones above, and the first open problem
[p32] of the 2025 survey [36, §8.1, P1] is to find ideal solutions of degree 10 and of every
[p32] degree at least 12. For the classical theory see Hua’s text [53, Ch. 18], the survey of
[p32] Dorwart and Brown [54] and [55, Ch. 11]. Ideal solutions, of size k + 1, are known
[p32] for the sizes n ≤ 10 and n = 12 [56], that is, for the degrees k ≤ 9 and k = 11, and
[p32] whether N (k ) = k + 1 for all k is open [4, §1].

[p33]     The proofs of (a)–(c) are in Appendix A, and (d) and (e) are proved after Propo-
[p33] sition 3.19; Theorem p1.2(ii) is (d) and (e). By Corollary 3.12(b), an orbifold of area
[p33] Apthat needs the ⌊ (A/2π − 1)/3⌋ + 2 invariants of (a) has a cone order at least
[p33] ⌊ (A/2π − 1)/3⌋ + 1: the growth in (a)–(d) is a growth of the cone orders, and with
[p33] orders at most M the count does not grow at all (Corollary 3.12(c)). With the known
[p33] quadratic bounds on N (k ), (b) gives exactly the exponent 12 of (a), and any exponent
[p33] above 12 in (d) would prove N (k ) = O(k 2−ϵ ). All of this passes through one quan-
[p33] tity. Let T (L) be the least size of an L-configuration and Amin (L) the infimum of the
[p33] areas of orbifolds O ∈ Sig with Kmult (O; Sig) ≥ L + 1, so that f (A) ≥ L + 1 for
[p33] A ≥ Amin (L), the infimum being attained (proof of Proposition 3.19), and f (A) ≤ L
[p33] for A < Amin (L).

[p33] The sandwich implies N (2L − 2) ≤ 4N (2L − 3), weaker than the classical doubling N (k + 1) ≤
[p33] 2N (k) [5]; the factor 4 is harmless for growth questions, and no new information on the
[p33] Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem is claimed. So, for large L, f is up to constant factors in A
[p33] the inverse function of T (the bounds on Amin carry additive constants), and f (A) = Θ(A),
[p33] T (L) = O(L) and N (k) = O(k) are equivalent.

[p34] reals is well ordered).                                                                      □
[p34]     So the extra structure of a configuration, odd exponents only and a vanishing
[p34] reciprocal sum, changes its least size against N by at most a constant factor: it cannot
[p34] by itself decide the growth exponent.

[p34]    An L-configuration of the least size 2L + 2 allowed by Theorem 3.7 is ideal, the
[p34] analogue of an ideal Prouhet–Tarry–Escott solution. The upper bounds below come
[p34] from one construction.
[p34] Lemma P 3.20 P   (Combining two pieces) Let P and B be finite multisets of nonzero rationals
[p34] with P z j = B z j = 0 for odd j ≤ 2L − 3 and with nonzero reciprocal sums ϱ(P ), ϱ(B).
[p34] Put τ = −ϱ(B)/ϱ(P ). Then P ⊎ τ B, with every pair {z, −z} removed, is an L-configuration
[p34] whenever it is nonempty and of even size; its size is at most |P | + |B| and its imbalance is
[p34] ι(P ) + sgn(τ ) ι(B).
[p34] Proof Odd power sums are homogeneous, so they vanish on τ B, and ϱ(τ B) = ϱ(B)/τ =
[p34] −ϱ(P ). Removing a pair {z, −z} changes no odd power sum, no reciprocal sum and no
[p34] imbalance, and scaling by τ < 0 reverses all signs.                              □
[p34]     Every pair of Example 3.21(iii) is of this form: each of its two pieces is an ideal
[p34] or symmetric solution of the Prouhet–Tarry–Escott problem, a pair of sets with equal
[p34] odd power sums, or (for L = 4 with equal cone counts) a set of seven integers whose
[p34]                                               34
[p34] 
[p35] odd power sums of degree at most 5 vanish; the pieces come from the literature, among
[p35] them the ideal symmetric solutions listed by Borwein and Ingalls [5, pp. 9, 25], three
[p35] of which they attribute to Letac and Gloden [5, p. 10], Gloden’s and Letac’s solutions
[p35] as given in [57, pp. 2064, 2069], the size-12 ideal symmetric solution of [56, (5)] and
[p35] equal sums of odd powers from [36, A.1.17, A.1.26, A.1.33], among them (A.313),
[p35] found by Chen in 2000, and (A.314)–(A.316), found by Wróblewski in 2009, according
[p35] to that survey; Table S1 of the supplement lists every piece. We did not search for
[p35] 4-configurations of size 12; 4-configurations of size 10 of the form A ∪ (−B ) with A,
[p35] B five-element integer sets with e1 = e3 = 0 and, after scaling B , equal e4 and e5 , do
[p35] not exist with entries at most 200 (a computer search; Section S8 of the supplement).
[p35] This search covers a narrow ansatz and says little about T (4) = 10, which would need
[p35] either an ideal configuration of type (−1, 1, 3, 5) of shape (5, 5), for which Chen gives
[p35] the identity [36, (3.33)] but no numerical solution, or a genus-changing configuration
[p35] of shape (4, 6).

[p36]        30 . (iii) L = 4, . . . , 7: genus-0 pairs with equal cone counts, |U | + |V | = 14, 18, 24, 40
[p36]                                                                   ∗       ∗
[p36] and area less than 2π · 5, 7, 10, 18, and genus pairs with |U | + |V | = 16, 20, 26, 40, each built
[p36] by Lemma 3.20 from the pieces listed after it.
[p36] Remark 3.22 (The first open case) Let Tg (L) be the least size of an L-configuration with
[p36] ι(Z) ̸= 0, that is, the least |U ∗ | + |V ∗ | over genus-changing pairs sharing L invariants;
[p36] Tg (2) = 6 (Example 3.9) and Tg (3) ≤ 10. By Theorem 3.15 a pair of size 8 has shape (3, 5),
[p36] and given its five-element side V , Newton’s identities determine the three-element side as the
[p36] root set of a cubic qV with coefficients rational in V (the equation for e3 has the coefficient
[p36] 3 − 3P1 (V )R(V ) ̸= 0, as P1 (V )R(V ) ≥ 25 by the inequality between the arithmetic and
[p36] harmonic means). A computer search with a modular test that rejects V only when qV cannot
[p36] split over Q (supplement, Sections S1 and S8(ii)) shows that no pair of size 8 has a five-
[p36] element side that, scaled by itself to coprime integers, has entries at most 220 (entries equal
[p36] to 1 included, and with the three-element side allowed to be any rational triple); the bound
[p36] concerns the five-element side alone. Real solutions of that shape exist, so an obstruction, if
[p36] any, is arithmetic. So Tg (3) ∈ {8, 10} is undecided, and whether the bound of Theorem 3.15
[p36] is attained for L = 3 is this question.
[p36] 4 What the expansion does not see, and two
[p36]   timescales
[p36] Heat invariants are local: up to an error smaller than every power of t, the heat trace is
[p36] a sum of contributions of arbitrarily small neighbourhoods of points [1], [2, Thm 4.8].
[p36] At curvature −1 this is visible in Theorem 2.3: I depends only on the area, E only
[p36] on the cone orders, and Hyp(t) = O(tK ) for every K (Lemma 2.5). So, by (7), every
[p36]                                                   36
[p36] 
[p37] cj (O) is a function of the signature, and two orbifolds of the same signature have the
[p37] same heat expansion to all orders. That heat invariants do not see the moduli follows
[p37] from the trace formula and is classical in substance [42, 58]; the form of the trace
[p37] formula used is that of [41, Rem. 2.7] and [59, proof of Thm 4.5]. This section gives the
[p37] consequence for Kiso and records with an explicit constant where the shape enters the
[p37] heat trace; what is new here is that constant, in terms of the systole and the diameter
[p37] (Proposition 4.2(a)), and the reading of the cone timescale through the wave trace
[p37] (Remark 4.3). The form of the constant without the diameter (Remark 2.6) rests on
[p37] a count of closed geodesics taken from the companion manuscript and is not claimed
[p37] here. The computed spectra then illustrate these proved statements: the expansions
[p37] of the minimal pair first differ in the third coefficient, at order t, because d3 = 25
[p37]                                                                                     12 ̸= 0,
[p37] and the shape enters only beyond all orders.
[p37] Corollary 4.1 (Moduli) Let O ∈ Sig have signature σ = (g; m1 , . . . , mn ).
[p37] (a) If O is a triangle orbifold, it is determined up to isometry by σ, so Kiso (O; Sig0,3 ) =
[p37]     Kmult (O; Sig0,3 ).
[p37]  (b) Otherwise the orbifolds of signature σ form uncountably many isometry classes, all with
[p37]      the same heat invariants, and Kiso (O; C) = ∞ for every class C containing them all (for
[p37]      example Sig, or Sig0,n when g = 0).
[p37] Proof Teich(O) ∼  = R6g−6+2n , the case of [40, Cor. 13.3.7], which gives the dimension
[p37] −3χ(XO ) + 2k + l for k cone points and l corner reflectors, with l = 0 and χ(XO ) = 2 − 2g,
[p37] and with n ≥ 0, 6g − 6 + 2n = 0 only for (g, n) ∈ {(1, 0), (0, 3)}, of which (1; ∅) is not hyper-
[p37] bolic. (a) For a triangle orbifold Teich(O) is a point: a hyperbolic triangle is determined
[p37] up to isometry by its angles, so the triangle group is unique up to conjugacy. (b) Points of
[p37] Teich(O) are classes of discrete faithful representations of Γ in P SL(2, R). Two of them give
[p37] isometric orbifolds exactly when they differ by an automorphism of Γ followed by a conju-
[p37] gation in P GL(2, R): an isometry of the quotients lifts to an isometry of H2 , an element of
[p37] P GL(2, R), which conjugates one group onto the other and so induces the automorphism. As
[p37] Γ is finitely generated, an automorphism is determined by the images of the generators, so
[p37] Aut(Γ) is countable; hence each isometry class meets Teich(O) in a countable set (an orbit
[p37] of the mapping class group of the orbifold), and a countable union of countable sets is not
[p37] Rd , d > 0.                                                                                    □

[p37] by ϑ = log(sinh a/ sinh b), with a, b the distances from the centre to two adjacent sides
[p37] (sinh a sinh b = 21 ). Its eight computed members, ϑ = 0, 0.4, . . . , 2.8, are pairwise non-
[p37] isometric: their computed first eigenvalues, from 4.122 down to 0.435, differ pairwise

[p39] Remark 4.3 (The wave trace) The two timescales of the next paragraphs have a simple
[p39] meaning
[p39] R            for the wave trace. Since ĝt is even, the elliptic term of Theorem 2.3 is Em (t) =
[p39]   R g t (u) k m (u) du with
[p39]                                             m−1
